 National Security Act of 1947, Public Law 235, 80<sup>th</sup> Cong., 1<sup>st</sup> sess. [July 26, 
1947], http://intelligence.senate.gov/nsaact1947.pdf (accessed June 8, 2011).
 <sup>2</sup>Noffsinger, *World War I Aviation Books in English: An Annotated Bibliography*
(Metuchen: Scarecrow, 1987), 2-3, 7-9, 18, 20, 26, 34, 41-43, 51, 53-54, 56, 58-
59, 70, 75-76, 78-79, 86-88, 94-95, 98, 100, 104-08, 111-12, 115, 117-19, 120, 
124, 127, 129-30, 132, 134, 139-42, 156, 158, 164-65, 170, 173, 177, 179, 185, 
205, 209, 224, 229-31, 240, 245, 252, 258, 263, 267, 269-71, 275-76, 278, 287, 
292.

---

Royal Air Force Bomber Command bombed Nazi targets from the air.<sup>3</sup> In front of 
the world the two air arms had tested theories that Douhet, Mitchell and 
Trenchard had preached during the inter-war decades and made at least part of 
the case for strategic bombing.  Civilian populations had not demanded that their 
government cease hostilities as strategic air power advocates had predicted, but 
the substitution of air forces for ground troops had served to keep American and 
British casualties low in comparison to the losses suffered by the Soviet armies.<sup>4</sup>
The Japanese capitulation after the Americans’ atomic bombings offered some 
vindication to air power theorists.  Military analysts and historians argued that 
aviation’s World War II success provided the model for future wars, allowing the 
United States to inflict maximum damage on an enemy while suffering minimal 
casualties.  Aerial bombing had taken over the lead in the American vision of war 
so convincingly it became possible to argue for the total “elimination of ground 
forces as major combatants in war” and that henceforth war might “be won 
entirely from the air.”   The vital auxiliary role reconnaissance and observation<sup>5</sup> 
crews had played during the earlier war vanished in the dust of Europe’s 
collapsed buildings and the atomic flashes that destroyed Hiroshima and 
Nagasaki.  Professional military analysts moved on to studies of World War II

<sup>3</sup>Adrian R. Lewis, *The American Culture of War: The History of U.S. Military 
Force from World War II to Operation Iraqi Freedom* (New York: Routledge, 
2007), 42-44.

<sup>4</sup>R.G. Price, “Casualties of War—Putting American Casualties in Perspective,” 
http://rationalrevolution.net/articles/casualties_of_war.htm (accessed June 25, 
2011) puts U.S. and U.S.S.R. military casualties in World War II at 295,000 and 
13,600,000 respectively; See also http://www.abmc.gov/search/wwii.php
(accessed June 25, 2011); www.secondworldwar.co.uk/index.php/fatalities
(accessed June 25, 2011).

5
Lewis, *American Culture of War,* 37.

---

largely taking professional historians with them.  The heavy emphasis on 
bombing inherent in the model of air power set during World War II prompted 
historians to reexamine aviation’s role in the Great War.  Judged against the 
great success bombing had enjoyed in that later war, the modest bombing 
programs of the First World War appeared miniscule, even quaint.  With bombing 
cast as the new *raison d’être* of military aviation—and few memorable bombing 
achievements to study from the years 1914-1918—historians began to minimize 
and even ignore aviation’s real contribution to the Great War.

More than recognition of bombing’s lackluster debut is required to 
understand why professional historians have neglected aviation in World War I. 
Professional historians’ pursuit of military analyses of Second World War studies 
left the field of World War I aviation research open for an enthusiastic group of 
amateurs.  Between 1959 and 1985 American, Australian and British amateur 
historians founded five journals dedicated to World War I aviation research, four 
of which remain in publication.   The relationship between these amateur<sup>6</sup> 
historians and their professional colleagues might also have discouraged 
professional interest.  In his book, *That Noble Dream,* historian Peter Novick 
alludes to an early-twentieth century rift between professional and amateur 
historians over book sales and a preference among the reading public for the

<sup>6</sup>H. Hugh Wynne, “Origins of the Organization,” *Cross & Cockade Journal* 1, no. 
1 (Spring 1960), 1; “About,” http://www.ww1aeroinc.org/about.html (accessed 
June 24, 2011); “The Australian Society of World War One Aero Historians, *The 
’14-18 Journal* Index: 1964-2004,”

http://asww1ah.0catch.com/Journal%20Index.pdf (accessed November 11, 
2008); *Cross & Cockade (Great Britain)* 1, no. 4 (Winter 1970), 84; “Let’s Go 
‘Over the Front,’” *Over the Front* 1, no. 1 (Spring 1986), 2.

---

popular style adopted by amateur history writers.   Professionals held their<sup>7</sup> 
amateur colleagues “in low regard” for their failure “to achieve a greater 
accuracy” and were frustrated by their own inability to break through to the 
general public.   Although Novick’s analysis focused on the first half of the<sup>8</sup> 
twentieth century, there is evidence to suggest professional bias against the 
amateur historian is still at work.  In a more recent essay discussing the 
relationship, historian Theodore Hamerow asserted that amateurs “have been 
cast into the outer darkness, dismissed by the professionals as dilettantes, 
popularizers and vulgarizers.”   The writing on World War I aviation history<sup>9</sup> 
suggests that the professional historians have largely left it to the amateurs.  
Although he characterizes the field as “vibrant,” David J. Fitzpatrick, in his
contribution to *Researching World War I: a Handbook,* includes a disappointingly 
brief list of recommended American scholarly aviation source material.<sup>10</sup>  Apart 
from autobiographies of leading American military aviation personalities William 
Mitchell and Edward Rickenbacker, Fitzpatrick fails to mention anything other 
than the work of the small handful of professional historians who have published 
in the field and ignores altogether the substantial body of research published by

<sup>7</sup>Peter Novick, *That Noble Dream: The “Objectivity Question” and the American 
Historical Profession* (Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1988), 193. 
 <sup>8</sup>Ibid., 194.

<sup>9</sup>Theodore S. Hamerow, “The Transformation of History into an Academic 
Discipline has Diminished its Role,” *The Chronicle of Higher Education* 34, no. 43 
(July 6, 1988), A40, quoted in Howard G. Fisher, “What’s Left to be Done: The 
First Thirty Years,” *Over the Front* 6, no. 2 (Summer 1991), 129.

<sup>10</sup>David J. Fitzpatrick, “United States: War in the Air,” in Robin Higham and 
Dennis E. Showalter, eds., *Researching World War I: a Handbook,* (Westport: 
Greenwood Press, 2003), 250.

---

the amateur community.<sup>11</sup>  Indeed, one historian has estimated that amateurs 
have contributed, “over ninety percent of World War I aviation material.”<sup>12</sup>

While the Great War was still in progress, before either the amateur or 
professional historians joined the conversation, aircrew, air commanders, and 
aviation technologists began writing about their field.  Autobiographical and 
biographical works made up one of the single largest categories of wartime 
offerings and these effectively influenced attitudes both toward aviation and the 
war generally.  And though substantial numbers of pilots and observers flew 
reconnaissance or bombing missions, most of the personal stories published 
were written by or about fighter pilots.<sup>13</sup>  Another large portion of the wartime 
literature answered the critical need for technical information.  These works are 
almost totally forgotten today due to their outdated scientific perspective, but at 
the time they were state of the art.<sup>14</sup>

<sup>11</sup>Ibid.,” 250-51.

<sup>12</sup>Fisher, “What’s Left to be Done,” 129.

<sup>13</sup>See chapter two.

In the decade following the war, while the world’s militaries debated the 
future of aviation, the general public continued to focus on the romantic nature of 
the air war.  In 1926, *Liberty Magazine* pulled itself from the brink of bankruptcy 
with publication of serial entitled *War Birds: the Diary of an Unknown Aviator.* 
The huge success of *War Birds* kept *Liberty Magazine* from sinking into financial 
insolvency and prompted its author, Elliott White Springs, to publish the complete 
series as a book and then follow it with seven very similar novels.<sup>15</sup>

Following the success of *War Birds, Liberty Magazine* lined up another 
Great War aviation biography. *The Red Knight of Germany* came from the wellestablished pen of American journalist Floyd Gibbons.  Gibbons work chronicled 
the wartime career and mysterious death of the conflict’s leading fighter ace, 
Manfred von Richthofen.  Gibbons’ Red Baron saga appeared over twenty-three 
installments during 1927 and, like *War Birds,* made the transition from magazine 
serial to book shortly thereafter.

Springs’ and Gibbons’ triumphs launched something of a surge in the 
fiction market.  Over the course of the dozen years separating the appearance of

(Garden City: Doubleday, Doran, 1928); Elliott White Springs, *Leave Me with a 
Smile* (Garden City: Doubleday, Doran, 1928); Elliott White Springs, *In the Cool 
of the Evening* (New York: E. Springs and Co., 1929); Elliott White Springs, 
*Contact* (New York: Sears Publishing, 1930); Elliott White Springs, *Pent Up on a 
Penthouse* (New York: E. Springs and Co., 1931); Elliott White Springs, *The Rise 
and Fall of Carol Banks* (Garden City: Doubleday, Doran, 1931).

*War Birds* and *The Red Knight of Germany* from the beginning of the Second 
World War in Europe, James Noffsinger documents the appearance of nearly 
sixty works and the beginning of several writing careers.<sup>16</sup>  A sampling of some of 
these titles illustrates the preoccupation with fighter pilots and adventure tales.<sup>17</sup>
The popularity of fictional World War I aviation books prompted a few non-fiction 
writers to try their hand at novels.  Charles Bernard Nordhoff and James Norman 
Hall, who had teamed up following the Armistice to write the two-volume history 
*The Lafayette Flying Corps,* entered the fiction market in 1929 with *Falcons of 
France,* an ostensibly fictional work that drew extensively from their own 
experiences in the war.<sup>18</sup>  Another veteran, Arthur George “Arch” Whitehouse, 
began his writing career in 1936 with *Wings of Adventure,* the first of an eventual 
twenty-three fictional and non-fictional accounts he wrote about flying in World 
War I.<sup>19</sup>

The sensation over *The Red Knight of Germany* and *War Birds* and the 
burgeoning fiction market came at a time when millions of Americans were going 
to the movies and Hollywood showed interest in putting at least one of the 
nationwide best sellers on film.  When production difficulties delayed the filming 
of *War Birds,* John Monk Saunders’, *Wings*—the first movie to win the “Best 
Picture” Oscar—hit theaters instead.<sup>20</sup> What Springs and Gibbons did for the 
Great War aviation book market, *Wings* did for movies about the war’s flying 
aces.  Before World War II interrupted the flow, Hollywood produced several 
blockbusters, including Howard Hughes’ multi-million dollar classic, *Hells Angels,* 
and two versions of *The Dawn Patrol.<sup>21</sup>*

---

Not to be outdone, pulp magazine publishers entered the First World War 
aviation market with great zeal, creating characters that refought the war with 
even more dash than the real war’s aces.  The most successful, Robert J. 
Hogan’s *G-8 and his Battle Aces,* lasted 110 issues published from October 1933 
to June 1944.  Hogan’s hero, *G-8,* fit well into the masculine image of aviators 
that existed in the minds of the public.   An American fighter pilot possessing 
nearly superhuman powers, *G-8* regularly battled all manner of evil villains, 
including extraterrestrials, to save the world from total annihilation.<sup>22</sup>

While thoroughly evil, *G-8’s* foes were not the only bogeymen haunting the 
1930s and reality could be much scarier than fantasy.  The success of fascism in 
Italy, the seizure of power by Nazis in Germany, and Francisco Franco’s victory 
in the Spanish Civil War—a victory brought about with significant assistance by a 
newly reactivated German *Luftwaffe*—shifted attention away from the leisurely 
pursuit of aviation history and fiction to more serious concerns.  In 1930 the 
British military historian Basil Liddell Hart offered his own dramatic contribution to 
the air power discussion and to the loss of the aerial reconnaissance story with 
his war chronicle, *The Real War, 1914-1918.<sup>23</sup>*  Liddell Hart’s analysis twists logic 
in a manner similar to Giulio Douhet in his interpretation of air power’s earliest

Vitaphone, 1930; *The Dawn Patrol,* directed by Edmund Goulding, Warner 
Brothers, 1938.

<sup>22</sup>Internet Archive, “G-8 and his Battle Aces—Squadron of Corpses,” 
www.archive.org/details/G8_vs_the_Squadron_of_Corpses (accessed October 
20, 2011); The Vintage Library, “G-8 and his Battle Aces,”

www.vintagelibrary.com/pulpfiction/characters/G-8-and-his-Battle-Aces.php
(accessed October 20, 2011).  Titles typical of the G-8 series include: *The Bat* 
*Staffel, The Purple Aces,* and *The Vampire Staffel.*

<sup>23</sup>Basil Liddell Hart, *The Real War, 1914-1918* (Boston: Little, Brown, 1930).

---

years.  The military analyst concedes the value of aerial intelligence collection, 
even going so far as to write that “to relate the action of aircraft in the military 
sphere is not possible, for it formed a thread running through and vitally 
influencing the whole course of operations, rather than a separate strategic 
feature.”<sup>24</sup>  Liddell Hart then condemns the effect aerial reconnaissance had on 
the war by maintaining its early maturation prevented senior commanders from 
adequately developing aviation’s more belligerent aspects in ways that would 
have brought the war to an early close.  The British historian wrote:

The very eagerness with which the armies had eventually 
embraced aircraft as immediate auxiliaries—for reconnaissance, 
artillery observation, and the protection of these duties—limited the 
supply of aircraft for roles of indirect cooperation and curtailed their 
exploitation of the bombing weapon.<sup>25</sup>

Liddell Hart’s argument assumes as fact an idea about bombing for which 
no proof has yet been offered, either by himself or by any other historian.  He 
speculates the damage that might have been done had the World War I 
belligerent powers developed more powerful bombers would have exceeded the 
damage actually done by artillery fire regulated by aerial observation and 
planned with the aid of aerial reconnaissance and photography.  Liddell Hart 
damned the war’s senior commanders for lacking the vision to appreciate what a 
larger investment in bombers might have done to the enemy’s industrial capacity 
to wage war instead of appreciating the tremendous impact they were able to

<sup>24</sup>Basil Liddell Hart, *Liddell Hart’s History of the First World War* (London:
Cassell, 1970), 355.

25
 Ibid., 358.

---

make by developing operational doctrine that properly utilized available 
equipment to the fullest extent then technologically possible.

Liddell Hart apparently came late to his air power theory.  When the first 
edition of *The Real War, 1914-1918* appeared in 1930 it did not in fact include 
the chapter on the air war he later entitled “Panaroma.”  Liddell Hart added the 
aviation section when reissuing the book in 1935 as *A History of the World War,* 
and the chapter remained in the 1970 edition, *Liddell Hart’s History of the World 
War.*  In the five years separating the first two editions the British military 
historian apparently developed sufficient belief in the value of military aviation to 
realize he needed to correct his earlier text.  His enhanced appreciation of the 
potential of air power, as well as his misunderstanding of reconnaissance within 
the broader topic, were likely influenced by several inter-war developments, 
including the debate over the future of military aviation that raged throughout the 
period sparked by Giulio Douhet’s *The Command of the Air* and the Billy Mitchell 
court martial, advances in the state of aviation technology demonstrated most 
dramatically by Charles Lindbergh’s Atlantic crossing, the rebirth of the *Luftwaffe*
in Germany, and the associated build up of European military aviation in 
anticipation of the Spanish Civil War.

Liddell Hart’s assessment of military aviation appeared at the beginning of 
a revival of the debate over the future of air power, an argument that had seemed to settle down in the wake of Billy Mitchell’s court-martial.<sup>26</sup>  While air power 
theorists continued their discussion, amateur and professional historians, 
novelists, and those writing memoirs temporarily retired from the field in 
preparation for the next war.  Further consideration of the first air war waited until 
the second concluded.

Bombing’s subsequent prominence in the Second World War seemed to 
prove what Liddell Hart had said about the First World War commanders’ failure 
to properly utilize aviation.  Armed with the theory that aviation could only 
seriously affect the course and outcome of war when it was dropping bombs on 
military, naval, or civilian targets, historians writing since the end of the Second 
World War have marched in the footsteps of Douhet and Liddell Hart ignoring 
aviation’s vital worth as an intelligence arm during World War I.

During the 1950s, World War I aeronautical history enjoyed a further 
resurgence in general circulation magazines and specialist periodicals.  Inspired 
by the success of *Air-Britain,* three California hobbyists founded the American 
Aviation Historical Society (AAHS) in January 1956.<sup>27</sup>  The *AAHS Journal’s*
comprehensive aviation focus failed to satisfy the group’s Great War aficionados 
and in August 1959 a handful spun off to form the Cross and Cockade Society of

<sup>26</sup>Henry Arnold, *This Flying Game* (New York: Funk and Wagnalls, 1936); Lionel

<sup>27</sup>Tim Williams with William T. Larkins, “The American Aviation Historical Society

accessed January, 24 2008.

---

World War I Aero Historians, an organization devoted solely to the study of flying 
in the Great War.  Publication of *Cross and Cockade Journal* began the following 
spring.<sup>28</sup>  That same year also witnessed the birth of *World War I Aero,* a 
quarterly magazine providing aircraft construction and colors-and-markings 
details to the modeling community.  In 1970 British *Cross and Cockade* members 
founded a sister organization to publish their own journal, *Cross and Cockade 
(Great Britain).<sup>29</sup> Windsock International,* a British modeling publication, joined 
the other quarterlies in 1985.  At the same time the Society of World War I Aero 
Historians ceased publication of the original *Cross and Cockade Journal.*  Its 
successor organization, the League of World War I Aviation Historians, began 
publication of *Over the Front* the following year.

The work the amateurs have produced has largely failed to explore aerial 
reconnaissance operations as well as the way in which these aerial operations 
connected to the broader war.  In his analysis of more than fourteen hundred 
articles published from 1960 through 1989 in *Cross and Cockade Journal, Cross 
and Cockade (Great Britain/International)* and *World War One Aero,* historian 
Howard Fisher confirms a strong preference for biographies, aircraft technology, 
colors-and-markings data, and other esoteric material.<sup>30</sup>  An update of Fischer’s 
initial review presented to the 2003 League of World War I Aviation Historians

28
 H. Hugh Wynne, “Origin of the Organization,” *Cross & Cockade Journal,* vol. 1, 
no.1, 1960, 1.

*<sup>29</sup>Cross and Cockade (Great Britain)* changed names to *Cross and Cockade 
International* following the demise of the original *Cross and Cockade Journal* in 
1985.

30
 Fisher, “What’s Left to be Done,” 130-33.

---

conference, suggests that the preferences of the amateur community have yet to 
change.<sup>31</sup>  Though Fisher did not include specific figures, an examination of the 
American biographical articles in *Cross and Cockade Journal* reveals forty-two of 
the eighty-nine subjects served as fighter pilots, while twenty-two worked as 
bomber crew, and a mere twelve as reconnaissance pilots, balloon observers, or 
rear-seat personnel.  A similar review of articles covering squadron histories 
showed a similar preference for fighter units.

The widespread appearance of personal computers in the 1990s drove 
historians toward the production of databases and quantitative comparisons, a 
development that continued to favor fighter aces.  *Above the Trenches,* the first 
of a successful series, chronicled the accomplishments of British fighter aces, 
complete with biographies and fully annotated victories lists for each.<sup>32</sup>  Acclaim 
for *Above the Trenches* spawned similar works detailing other country’s fighter 
aces, but not their reconnaissance crews.<sup>33</sup>  The data published then expanded

photography, or reconnaissance that would permit aerial reconnaissance crews 
to showcase their contributions to the war in the same way fighter pilots and 
bomber crews document their own successes.

---

Aviation historians’ lack of attention to aerial reconnaissance cannot be 
explained by an aversion to the obscure.  Over the decades several esoteric 
topics have been exhaustively examined.  The two most significant of these have 
involved the life and death of Germany’s top fighter ace, Manfred von Richthofen, 
and the study of aircraft colors and markings.  Richthofen has been the subject of 
more research than any other single World War I aviation personality with no sign 
interest is likely to soon subside.<sup>37</sup>

<sup>37</sup>Floyd Gibbons’s *The Red Knight of Germany* (Garden City: Doubleday, Page 
and Co., 1927); Heinz Nowarra and Kimborough S. Brown, *von Richthofen and 
the Flying Circus* (Letchworth: Harleyford Publications, 1958); “Vigilant” (Claude 
Sykes), *Richthofen, the Red Knight of the Air* (1934; repr., Bath: Cedric Chivers 
Ltd., 1967); Manfred von Richthofen, *The Red Air Fighter* (1918; repr., Bath: 
Cedric Chivers Ltd., 1967); Floyd Gibbons, *El Caballero Rojo de Alemania*
(Madrid: Libreria Sagasta, 1969); P. J. Carisella’s and James D. Ryan’s *Who 
Killed the Red Baron?: the Final Answer* (Wakefield: Daedalus Publishing, 1969);
Peter Kilduff, *The Red Baron* (NY: Doubleday and Co., 1969); Dale Titler, *The 
Day the Red Baron Died* (NY: Ballantine Books, 1970); *von Richthofen and 
Brown,* directed by Roger Corman, Corman and Company, 1971; Norman
Franks, Hal Giblin, and Nigel McCrery, *Under the Guns of the Red Baron: the 
Complete Record of von Richthofen’s Victories and Victims Fully Illustrated*
(London: Grub Street, 1995); Peter Kilduff, *Richthofen: Beyond the Legend of the

von Richthofen No. 1* (London: Grub Street, 1996); Norman Franks and Alan

*The Red Baron Combat Wing: Jagdgeschwader Richthofen in Battle* (London: 
Arms and Armour Press, 1998); Peter Kilduff, *Illustrated Red Baron: the Life and 
Times of Manfred von Richthofen* (London: Arms and Armour Press, 1999); 
Suzanne Hayes Fischer, *Mother of Eagles: the War Diary of Baroness von 
Richthofen* (Atglen: Schiffer Military Publishing, 2001); Frank McGuire, *The Many 
Deaths of the Red Baron: the Richthofen Controversy 1918-2000* (Calgary: 
Bunker to Bunker Publishing, 2001); Peter Kilduff, *Talking with the Red Baron:* 
*Interviews with Manfred von Richthofen* (London: Brassey’s, 2003); Peter Kilduff,

Charles, 2007); *The Red Baron,* directed by Nikolai Müllerschön, Niama Film, 
2008.

---

The study of aircraft heraldry began in earnest in 1960 with publication of 
an article by H.D. Hastings and Paul Parker, Jr., “Found: The Lost Colors of 
Germany’s War Birds,” which the pair expanded to a full book in 1965.<sup>38</sup>  Since 
the original *Cavalier* article appeared aircraft researchers have regularly included 
colors-and-markings material in their publications.<sup>39</sup>  Interest in the field seems 
insatiable, the highly speculative nature of attempting to discern accurate color 
descriptions from black-and-white photographs notwithstanding.<sup>40</sup>

Amateur World War I aviation historians’ general preference for minutia 
has compartmentalized aerial operations apart from the broader history of the 
First World War.  Edward Jablonski opines such bifurcation has managed to 
“produce a history of the war in the air and make it appear that it was the only 
one being fought during 1914-18.”<sup>41</sup>

While the issue of aviation’s diminution has seriously impacted the 
historiography, if the matter disappeared entirely and historians analyzing ground 
or naval warfare acknowledged aviation’s major role in World War I they would

<sup>38</sup>H. D. Hastings and Paul Parker, Jr., “Found: The Lost Colors of Germany’s 
War Birds,” *Cavalier,* December 1960, 32-34, 62-64; H. D. Hastings and Paul 
Parker, Jr., *Warplanes in Battle Dress 1914-1918,* (NY: Waller and Co., 1965).
<sup>39</sup>J. M. Bruce, *The S.E. 5A* (Leatherhead: Profile Publications, 1965); Raymond 
L. Rimell, *Albatros D.III: Windsock Datafile No. 1* (Berkhamsted: Albatros 
Productions, Ltd., 1986).  Beginning with these titles Profile Publications issued a 
total of forty Great War aircraft monographs and Albatros Productions started a 
series that, as of this writing, has reached one hundred forty-nine issues.  Each 
*Profile* and *Windsock Datafile* contains color illustrations.

<sup>40</sup>Peter M. Grosz, George Haddow, and Peter Schiemer, *Austro-Hungarian Army 
Aircraft of World War One* (Mountain View: Flying Machines Press, 1993), 469.

<sup>41</sup>Edward Jablonski, *The Knighted Skies: A Pictorial History of World War I in the 
Air* (New York: Putnam, 1964), 73.

---

still find their studies hampered by the improper categorization of reconnaissance 
and observation within the overall concept of air power.  Rather than view aerial 
intelligence gathering as a division of military aviation, both amateur and 
professional historians have frequently written about reconnaissance and 
observation as an early stage in the evolution of air power.  Edward Jablonski 
writes in 1964 that in the war’s early months “the major function of planes 
remained scouting and reconnaissance.”<sup>42</sup>  By the end of 1914, however, 
Jablonski adds:

some airmen—and some, but fewer, ground men—were awakening 
to the airplane’s possibilities as an instrument of destruction.  The 
offhand, almost hit-or-miss operations in which the airplane had 
engaged began to intimate a future role in the war.  The fledglings, 
having sprouted more powerful wings, were growing talons.<sup>43</sup>

Few subsequent mentions of reconnaissance and observation appear in 
Jablonski’s account.  He writes almost exclusively about fighter pilots and 
Zeppelin raids, arguing “during World War I, air power was not conclusive,” at 
least not in the way aviation contributed to the successful conclusion of World 
War II.<sup>44</sup>  Considering some of the other functions aviation fulfilled between 1914 
and 1918, he characterizes “observation” and “photography” as “limitedly 
effective in a military sense.”<sup>45</sup>  Jablonski gave the observation crews a brief 
opportunity to counter this argument by quoting American observation pilot, Lt. 
Walter V. Barneby, who wrote to his mother “it is the observation planes that do 
most of the hard work and get the least credit for it.  They are the eyes of the

<sup>42</sup>Ibid., 25.

<sup>43</sup>Ibid., 29.

<sup>44</sup>Ibid., 73.

45
 Ibid.

---

modern army, and their work is by far the most important.”<sup>46</sup>  While Barneby’s 
claim might have impressed his mother, it failed to convince Jablonski, who 
concluded that, “the first war in the air was, in the main, a fighter pilot’s war.”<sup>47</sup>

Just as Jablonski’s popular history writes off the immediate value of 
reconnaissance and observation in favor of the more lethal potential seen in 
bombers and fighters, so do other more scholarly works.  Irwin B. Holley takes 
this same path in his 1971 study of U.S. Air Service doctrine and technology.  
Holley argued that Brig. Gen. George Scriven’s December 1914 appearance 
before the House Military Affairs Committee, during which the Chief Signal 
Officer told Congress he did not believe airplanes had proven their worth as 
weapons, “exposed his failure to distinguish between the concept of the aerial 
weapon and its contemporary application.” Holley went on to speculate that the 
“probable span between existing technological development and ultimate 
development escaped him.”<sup>48</sup>  In sum, aerial reconnaissance constituted only the 
beginning and the true value of aviation lie in its ability to evolve beyond its use 
as an elevated observation platform.

This failure of air power disciples and amateur historians properly to 
characterize the status of aerial intelligence gathering during World War I is an 
important key to understanding why the airplane’s role has been written out of

<sup>46</sup>Ibid., 203-04.

<sup>47</sup>Ibid., 217.

<sup>48</sup>Irwin B. Holley, Jr., *Ideas and Weapons: Exploitation of the Aerial Weapon by 
the United States during World War I; a Study in the Relationship of* 
*Technological Advance, Military Doctrine, and the Development of Weapons*
(Hamden: Archon Books, 1971), 31.

---

the war’s larger history. Appreciating aviation’s value is impossible without a 
thorough understanding of the worth of aerial reconnaissance and observation to 
ground and naval operations. Furthermore, historians, like military strategists, 
cannot adequately appreciate or communicate the true nature of the war without 
incorporating the role played by aviation into their narratives.  Nor can air power 
students reach a complete understanding of their own discipline if they continue 
to minimize observation and reconnaissance; a branch that constitutes one third 
of the field and which clearly dominated its early years.

The Smithsonian’s National Air and Space Museum lends its considerable 
institutional authority to the diminution of the role aerial reconnaissance played in 
the first air war in the “Legend, Memory and the Great War in the Air” Gallery, 
part of the museum’s main facility on the national mall.  The aircraft on display 
point the visitor’s attention toward the romantic image of the fighter pilot rather 
than the utility of aerial reconnaissance and the story told in the accompanying 
explanatory material depicts aviation as a failure.<sup>49</sup>  Courtland Bryan, author of 
the “authorized book” on the museum’s collection sums up the part aviation 
played in the fighting by contending:

Most historians agree that the role played by air power during this 
struggle was more romantic than decisive.  The most important 
missions the airplane carried out were reconnaissance and artillery 
fire control.  And when the war bogged down into static frontline

<sup>49</sup>“Legend, Memory and the Great War in the Air,”

www.nasm.si.edu/exhibitions/gal206/index.cfm (accessed June 27, 2011) 
catalogues five single-seat fighters and one multi-seat bomber present in the 
gallery.

---

trench warfare and increasingly effective camouflage techniques 
evolved, aerial reconnaissance became less and less significant.<sup>50</sup>

Bryan’s interpretation of aviation’s diminishing importance over the course 
of the war does not make sense against overwhelming evidence that all the all 
the belligerent powers continually expanded their air services between 1914 and 
1918.  More than twenty million visitors passed through the National Air and 
Space Museum in the two years following its 1976 opening and more recent 
attendance figures still exceed five million annual visitors, making it the second 
most popular museum in the United States.<sup>51</sup>  Numbers this large suggest the 
museum’s misleading presentation must contribute significantly to the public’s 
perception of World War I aviation history.

Descriptions of the airplane in its intelligence-gathering role are typically 
found in the first few pages of aviation histories or in the “air war” chapter of the 
war’s general histories.  John Toland’s Pulitzer Prize-winning *No Man’s Land:* 
*1918 – the Last Year of the Great War* discusses aviation sporadically, only 
occasionally mentioning reconnaissance and observation.<sup>52</sup>  Toland notes, for 
example, that in the run-up to Germany’s March 1918 “Operation Michael” 
offensive, Hindenberg hoped bad weather would keep enemy reconnaissance

<sup>50</sup>Courtland Bryan, *The National Air and Space Museum* (New York: Abrams, 
1979), 2, 171.

<sup>51</sup>Ibid., 2; Rob Baedeker, “America’s 25 Most Visited Museums,” 
www.usatoday.com/travel/destinations/2007-09-28-most-visited-museums-
<u>forber&#95;N.htm</u> (accessed June 27, 2011).

<sup>52</sup>John Toland, *No Man’s Land: 1918 – the Last Year of the Great War* (New 
York: Smithmark, 1980).

---

flights on the ground.<sup>53</sup>  He also tells his readers that later in the summer, during 
the Battle of Chateau Thierry, William Mitchell, then a U.S. Air Service Colonel,
flew over the front drinking in the details of the ground fighting.<sup>54</sup>  As Toland 
builds to the final American offensive at the Meuse-Argonne, he is ready to admit 
“the safety of thousands of our attacking soldiers” rode on the success of the 
U.S. Air Service’s attacks on German observation balloons.<sup>55</sup>  But, like many 
other historians, Toland does not discuss in any depth the role those balloons, or 
the airplanes engaged in reconnaissance, played in operational planning, or the 
value of aerial observation to artillery operations or infantry liaison.  Toland 
preferred instead to concentrate on fighter pilots such as Eddie Rickenbacker, 
Manfred von Richthofen, and Quentin Roosevelt, or the famous American air 
commander, Billy Mitchell.<sup>56</sup>

In 1993 Lyn MacDonald’s *1915: the Death of Innocence* devotes the 
equivalent of just a couple of its 601 pages to the Royal Flying Corps’s early 
photography and artillery registration efforts.  MacDonald writes that “the 
mapmakers were working overtime and for the first time since the stalemate had 
set in the Army would have eyes, would be able to see beyond ridges and round 
corners, and the troops preparing for the coming battle would know precisely 
where they were going and what they would be up against.”<sup>57</sup>  Flattering though

<sup>53</sup>Ibid.,10.

<sup>54</sup>Ibid., 318-19.

<sup>55</sup>Ibid., 431.

<sup>56</sup>Ibid., 10, 64, 183-86, 314, 317-18, 424, 431-32, 436, 470.

<sup>57</sup>Lyn MacDonald, *1915: the Death of Innocence* (London: Headline Book 
Publishing, 1993), 70.

---

they appear, these few words hardly qualify as a thorough and sifting discussion 
of the revolution addition of the third dimension brought to battle planning.  
Further, MacDonald’s otherwise lengthy bibliography does not contain a single 
aviation title, not even a reference to Raleigh and Jones’s *The War in the Air,* 
despite MacDonald’s mention of several other volumes of the British *Official 
History.*

In their account of the United States’ war effort, *The Last Days of 
Innocence: America at War, 1917 – 1918,* Meirion and Susie Harries 
acknowledge a variety of possible uses of air power, including reconnaissance, 
observation and photography, before focusing heavily on aerial combat.  In part, 
they also offer support for Adrian Lewis’s and Linda Robertson’s arguments that 
Americans believe it possible to fight wars at a safe distance by substituting air 
power for ground troops, thereby saving countless American lives.<sup>58</sup>  The Harries 
begin by writing that aircraft played “an important part in the battle, observing, 
photographing, directing artillery, strafing ground troops, bombing rear areas, 
bombing civilian targets . . . dropping propaganda leaflets, and fighting to deny 
the same opportunities to the enemy.”<sup>59</sup>  Forced to observe the war from a 
distance until 1917, Americans apparently came to regard aerial warfare as an 
attractive alternative to more traditional forms of battle.  The Harries argue in a 
similar direction to Robertson when they speculate “this could be America’s kind

<sup>58</sup>Lewis, *American Culture of War,* 37; Linda Robertson, *The Dream of Civilized 
Warfare* (Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 2003), xii.

<sup>59</sup>Merrion Harries and Susie Harries, *The Last Days of Innocence: America at 
War, 1917-1918* (New York: Random House, 1997), 149.

---

of combat with dogfights between aces offering far more scope for rugged 
individualism than the dirty, bloody, depersonalized struggle on the ground.”<sup>60</sup>
From this focus on the romance rather than the substance of the air war, the 
Harries follow development of the U.S. Air Service on the Western Front through 
its climax in the Meuse Argonne campaign, during which they maintain, “Billy 
Mitchell’s aircraft at last began to fulfill some of the soaring hopes of the apostles 
of air power.”  At the “very last moment the air forces were putting the ‘Yankee 
Punch’ into the war and pointing the way to the air combat of the future.”<sup>61</sup>
Acknowledging the importance of reconnaissance to a greater degree than other 
historians, the Harrieses, like their contemporaries, relegate aviation’s “eyes of 
the army” function to the lesser status of an early development.  By implication 
they pigeonhole reconnaissance as a steppingstone along the path to the more 
aggressive and more important work that followed.

Similarly, only a few lines of 427 pages are devoted to the role aircraft and 
airmen played in the war in John Keegan’s, *The First World War,* a wide-ranging 
and influential account of the conflict published in 1998.  Offering this opinion on 
the state of air power in the penultimate year of the fighting, Keegan concludes, 
“The war in the air, which by 1918 would take a dramatic leap forward into the 
fields of ground attack and long-range strategic bombing, remained during 1917

<sup>60</sup>Ibid.

61
 Ibid., 416-17.

---

largely stuck at the level of artillery observation, ‘balloon busting,’ and dogfighting 
to gain or retain air superiority.”<sup>62</sup>

Richard Holmes’s biography of Sir John French, *The Little Field Marshal,* 
reserves most of its discussion of aviation for French’s stint as commander of 
British home defense efforts.  As part of this assignment French had charge of 
protecting Great Britain from aerial attack.  Attempting to coordinate Army and 
Navy programs for protection of the homeland made French a believer in, and an 
advocate for, a unified air command and an independent air force.  The British 
Field Marshal also became an early champion of the idea a modern air force 
could substitute for a larger, traditional ground army.  As Lord Lieutenant of 
Ireland, the post he took on following his period as chief home defender, French 
advocated the liberal use of force against Sinn Fein.  As Holmes writes, “French 
was well aware that the lack of trained troops in Ireland made the sort of action 
he envisaged somewhat difficult to achieve.  He had his own ideas as to how this 
shortage could best be offset.  On 14 April 1918 he had decided that aircraft 
could be used with good effect in Ireland.”<sup>63</sup>  Making this argument while the war 
was still in progress places French ahead of Trenchard and Mitchell as an air 
power advocate, as neither of the famous military aviation leaders took up this 
cause until after the war.  Holmes’s emphasis on home defense and imperial 
policing might imply that French thought that the Royal Flying Corps’s 
observation work in France had little value.  Yet no less an authority than the

<sup>62</sup>John Keegan, *The First World War* (New York: Knopf, 1998), 359.

<sup>63</sup>Richard Holmes, *The Little Field Marshal: A Life of Sir John French* (London: 
Cassell, 2004), 352.

---

aerial photography pioneer, Lt. Col. John Moore-Brabazon, put French at the 
forefront of reliance on aerial reconnaissance, noting that, “at the end of 1914, 
General French asked General [David] Henderson [first commanding general of 
the Royal Flying Corps in the field] to start taking photographs from the air.”<sup>64</sup>

These general histories of the war portray air power as an evolutionary 
process in which the importance of aerial reconnaissance diminished in direct 
relation to the growth of an air force’s more combative arms.  In their view the 
airplane’s more aggressive and more glamorous functions simply overshadowed 
the mundane duty of gathering intelligence until reconnaissance and observation 
simply stepped out of the story altogether.  Their suggestions that the limited 
performance, overall unreliability, lack of impressive weight-carrying capability, 
and total lack of armament of early military aircraft restricted their usefulness to 
the performance of reconnaissance carry with them the implication 
reconnaissance itself was of little or no military value.

Disconnecting the air war from the rest of the fighting, the failure of 
amateur and professional historians to properly characterize reconnaissance as 
a division of air power, and the restructuring of military aviation following the 
Second World War to emphasize strategic bombing have all combined to the 
detriment of the reputation of the Great War’s aerial reconnaissance fliers.  And 
in the absence of a thorough discussion of the worth of aerial reconnaissance 
historians analyzing the conflict from a broader perspective have minimized

<sup>64</sup>John Moore-Brabazon to Charles Fairbairn, October 26, 1918, Moore-
Brabazon Papers, RAF Museum, London.

---

aviation’s importance to the point that the whole field has been practically written 
out of the war’s overall story.  The real significance of aviation in the First World 
War, now routinely ignored, lies in how quickly and how thoroughly aerial 
observation, photography, and reconnaissance became integral parts of combat 
on the ground through their connections to artillery spotting, mapmaking and 
intelligence gathering.  From the very beginning aviation dictated the direction 
taken by the fighting in ways that lengthened the conflict while simultaneously 
adding to the casualties and saving lives.  Aviators created the conditions that led 
to positional warfare on the Western Front and destroyed the initial Russian effort 
against Germany’s eastern frontier.  Later their observation missions extended 
the possible range of artillery well beyond early efforts at indirect fire that 
previously depended on unsuitable maps and ground-based observers.  During 
the war’s last two years the development of naval convoys equipped with 
balloons and covered by seaplane and flying boat patrols defeated the U-Boat 
threat.  In the final phase of the Middle East campaign, aircrews proved their 
worth by returning quickly with vital information from terrain impervious to cavalry 
patrols.<sup>65</sup>  Aviators had become vital to the war.

The development of three-dimensional warfare magnified the battlefield 
bringing greater numbers of troops and entire civilian populations into the

<sup>65</sup>H.A. Jones, *The War in the Air: Being the Story of the Part Played in the Great 
War by the Royal Air Force* (London: Oxford, 1935) 5:240-41.  During the final 
Turkish retreat, the official air historian credits No. 67 (Australian) Squadron’s 
photographic section, with the help of a special staff at Weli Sheikh Nuran, with 
providing army staffs with prints made from plates exposed over the Turkish lines 
less than four to five hours earlier.

---

fighting, turning what most had expected to be a short war of maneuver into a 
grueling endurance contest of attrition the Germans called *Materialschlacht.*  By 
putting eyes all over the sky aviators not only altered the character of the First 
World War, they changed the nature and scope of organized warfare forever.
